Well, um... maybe a little? But I don't make a big deal out of it, I promise. Most days I just... I take the stairs at my apartment, and on the job sites too. I usually just tell people I'm trying to get some extra steps in, you know? It's good exercise, so it's not like it's a bad thing. 

But, um... if an elevator or a hoist is literally the only way up to a floor... I usually try to just ask the foreman if there's different work I can do on a lower level. I always make sure I'm still working hard when I do that, though! I never just sit around. Or, if I absolutely have to ride it, I'll... well, I'll only go if somebody I really trust is standing right there in the car with me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um... I guess it gets a little intense, but it's really not the worst thing in the world, you know? I try to keep it together. But, um... as soon as those doors start sliding shut, it's like... my chest just gets really tight. And my hands start sweating, which is embarrassing, especially if I'm around the crew. 

I just... all I can think is, 'I need out,' even if we're only going up, like, a few floors. I know it's silly because it's just a short ride, and I always tell myself I'm fine. But in that moment, my body just kind of panics a little bit. 

I hope that doesn't make me sound weak or anything. I really do try to hide it so I don't bother anyone else. Is that a normal way to react when you're scared of something?

Well, um... I guess I do sometimes? But I try not to let it ruin my whole day or anything! Like, most days, I just take the stairs at my apartment building, and I do the same thing on job sites. It's actually really good exercise, you know? 

If I have to go somewhere like a hospital or a mall, I'll usually just look for the stairs right away. And, um, if an elevator or a construction lift is absolutely the only way up... I might ask the foreman if I can do different work on the ground instead. Or... well, if I really, really have to ride it, I'll only do it if somebody I trust is standing right there in the car with me. 

I hope that doesn't sound too needy or annoying for the other guys on the crew. I really try to just handle it quietly so I'm not making a big fuss. Taking the stairs is a pretty healthy choice anyway, don't you think?

I just don't want to be a bother to anyone else if I get nervous.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid going to certain places, like shopping malls or hospitals, because they have elevators?\n\nPatient: Well, um... And, um, if an elevator or a construction lift is absolutely the only way up... I might ask the foreman if I can do different work on the ground instead. Or... well, if I really, really have to ride it, I'll only do it if somebody I trust is standing right there in the car with me. \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too needy or annoying for the other guys on the crew. I really try to just handle it quietly so I'm not making a big fuss. Taking the stairs is a pretty healthy choice anyway, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of elevators is interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I'm really staying on top of my responsibilities! \n\nIt's just... I guess taking the stairs all the time does make me a little late sometimes, or I show up to my shift already pretty worn out from climbing. I don't want anyone to think I'm slacking off or that I can't handle my life. It's just a small bump in the road, right? Lots of people have little things they have to work around, don't they?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt like you're missing out on important experiences because of your fear of elevators?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, um... no, I don't think so. I really don't feel like I'm missing out on anything big. I mean, my life is pretty full with work and my classes, and I'm really happy with how things are going!

I really do try my best to just work around it without bothering anyone.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you are unable to avoid riding an elevator?\n\nPatient: Well, um... Oh, um... no, I wouldn't say that. I really like the jobs I get! I mean, construction is mostly outside or on lower levels anyway, especially the kind of labor I do. So it's not like I'm turning down all these amazing opportunities or anything.

But in that moment, my body just kind of panics a little bit. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound weak or anything. I really do try to hide it so I don't bother anyone else. Is that a normal way to react when you're scared of something?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a job or an opportunity because it involved working in a building with elevators as the primary means of access?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I have, maybe once or twice? But I promise I'm still working really hard! I still go to all my shifts, and I'm still making it to my GED classes at night. I never just skip out on my responsibilities, you know? \n\nIt's just... taking the stairs everywhere can make me a little bit late sometimes, or I get pretty worn out before I even start my shift. And, um, I guess if I have to turn down a job because it's only accessible by an elevator or a hoist, it... it probably does make me a little less useful to the foreman on certain sites. \n\nI feel really bad about it, I do. I try to make up for it by working extra hard on the ground level stuff! I hope that's okay? Like, I'm still doing my best, I'm just... working around it a little bit. That doesn't make me a terrible worker, does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided social or professional events because they were being held in a location that you knew would require you to use an elevator?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really? I mean, I don't think I've ever skipped out on something important just because of that. I really try to be there for things, especially if it's for work or if friends invite me out. \n\nI might, um... I might just check ahead of time to see if there are stairs I can use instead? And if it's way up high, I'll usually just say I have a conflict or I'm too tired from work, which is kind of true sometimes! But I don't just sit at home avoiding people, I promise. I always try to make it work if I can. That's a pretty normal way to handle it, right? I just don't want to be a bother to anyone else if I get nervous.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid going to certain places, like shopping malls or hospitals, because they have elevators?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I do sometimes? But I try not to let it ruin my whole day or anything! Like, most days, I just take the stairs at my apartment building, and I do the same thing on job sites. It's actually really good exercise, you know? \n\nIf I have to go somewhere like a hospital or a mall, I'll usually just look for the stairs right away. And, um, if an elevator or a construction lift is absolutely the only way up... I might ask the foreman if I can do different work on the ground instead. Or... well, if I really, really have to ride it, I'll only do it if somebody I trust is standing right there in the car with me. \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too needy or annoying for the other guys on the crew. I really try to just handle it quietly so I'm not making a big fuss. Taking the stairs is a pretty healthy choice anyway, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of elevators is interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess maybe a little bit? But I don't want it to sound like I'm falling apart or anything! Like I said, I'm still getting to all my construction shifts, and I haven't missed a single GED class. I'm really staying on top of my responsibilities! \n\nIt's just... I guess taking the stairs all the time does make me a little late sometimes, or I show up to my shift already pretty worn out from climbing. And, um, turning down those jobs where you have to use a hoist or an elevator... It's really not a big deal to them, and it doesn't bother me either. \n\nI guess maybe I missed a couple of rooftop parties over the years, but I'm not really a big party person anyway, you know? I'd much rather just have a quiet night in or hang out somewhere easy. So I don't think it's holding me back from living a good life. That makes sense, right? I just wake up a tiny bit earlier, and then everything goes totally smooth. \n\nI really try to just blend in and do what I need to do without letting it mess up my schedule. I usually try to just ask the foreman if there's different work I can do on a lower level. I always make sure I'm still working hard when I do that, though! I never just sit around. Or, if I absolutely have to ride it, I'll... well, I'll only go if somebody I really trust is standing right there in the car with me. \n\nThat's not too crazy, right? I just feel better if someone is there. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee or anything... I really do try my best to just work around it without bothering anyone.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you are unable to avoid riding an elevator?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it gets a little intense, but it's really not the worst thing in the world, you know? I try to keep it together. But, um... as soon as those doors start sliding shut, it's like... my chest just gets really tight. And my hands start sweating, which is embarrassing, especially if I'm around the crew. \n\nI just... all I can think is, 'I need out,' even if we're only going up, like, a few floors. I know it's silly because it's just a short ride, and I always tell myself I'm fine. But in that moment, my body just kind of panics a little bit. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound weak or anything. I really do try to hide it so I don't bother anyone else. Is that a normal way to react when you're scared of something?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a job or an opportunity because it involved working in a building with elevators as the primary means of access?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I have, maybe once or twice? But I promise I'm still working really hard! I still go to all my shifts, and I'm still making it to my GED classes at night. I never just skip out on my responsibilities, you know? \n\nIt's just... taking the stairs everywhere can make me a little bit late sometimes, or I get pretty worn out before I even start my shift. And, um, I guess if I have to turn down a job because it's only accessible by an elevator or a hoist, it... it probably does make me a little less useful to the foreman on certain sites. \n\nI feel really bad about it, I do. I try to make up for it by working extra hard on the ground level stuff! I hope that's okay? Like, I'm still doing my best, I'm just... working around it a little bit. That doesn't make me a terrible worker, does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided social or professional events because they were being held in a location that you knew would require you to use an elevator?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really? I mean, I don't think I've ever skipped out on something important just because of that. I really try to be there for things, especially if it's for work or if friends invite me out. \n\nI might, um... I might just check ahead of time to see if there are stairs I can use instead? And if it's way up high, I'll usually just say I have a conflict or I'm too tired from work, which is kind of true sometimes! But I don't just sit at home avoiding people, I promise. I always try to make it work if I can. That's a pretty normal way to handle it, right? I just don't want to be a bother to anyone else if I get nervous.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid going to certain places, like shopping malls or hospitals, because they have elevators?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I do sometimes? But I try not to let it ruin my whole day or anything! Like, most days, I just take the stairs at my apartment building, and I do the same thing on job sites. It's actually really good exercise, you know? \n\nIf I have to go somewhere like a hospital or a mall, I'll usually just look for the stairs right away. And, um, if an elevator or a construction lift is absolutely the only way up... I might ask the foreman if I can do different work on the ground instead. Or... well, if I really, really have to ride it, I'll only do it if somebody I trust is standing right there in the car with me. \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too needy or annoying for the other guys on the crew. I really try to just handle it quietly so I'm not making a big fuss. Taking the stairs is a pretty healthy choice anyway, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of elevators is interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess maybe a little bit? But I don't want it to sound like I'm falling apart or anything! Like I said, I'm still getting to all my construction shifts, and I haven't missed a single GED class. I'm really staying on top of my responsibilities! \n\nIt's just... I guess taking the stairs all the time does make me a little late sometimes, or I show up to my shift already pretty worn out from climbing. And, um, turning down those jobs where you have to use a hoist or an elevator... I suppose it does make me a little less useful to the foreman on certain assignments. \n\nBut I'm really trying my best to make up for it in other ways! I don't want anyone to think I'm slacking off or that I can't handle my life. It's just a small bump in the road, right? Lots of people have little things they have to work around, don't they?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're missing out on important experiences because of your fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: Well, um... no, I don't think so. I really don't feel like I'm missing out on anything big. I mean, my life is pretty full with work and my classes, and I'm really happy with how things are going! \n\nIf my friends want to do something, we usually just go to places on the ground floor anyway, like a diner or a park. And if there's ever a time we need to go up, I just find the stairs. It's really not a big deal to them, and it doesn't bother me either. \n\nI guess maybe I missed a couple of rooftop parties over the years, but I'm not really a big party person anyway, you know? I'd much rather just have a quiet night in or hang out somewhere easy. So I don't think it's holding me back from living a good life. That makes sense, right? I think I'm doing okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of elevators has caused you to significantly change your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, definitely not significantly. I mean, my routine is pretty much just like anyone else's, I think! I'm definitely not! I just try to be practical. That's a good way to look at it, isn't it? I really like the jobs I get! I mean, construction is mostly outside or on lower levels anyway, especially the kind of labor I do. I might look for a different site? But there's always plenty of work around, so it's not like I'm limiting myself. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, no, I wouldn't say it's limited my advancement, exactly. I mean, I'm still working hard, getting to my job sites, going to my GED classes at night... I'm really trying to do everything right!

I guess... like I was saying before, having to take the stairs all the time, or passing on those jobs where you have to take the lift up... it does mean I get there a little late sometimes, or I'm already out of breath before the real work starts. So maybe I can't take on certain roles that need someone up top right away, and I might not be quite as useful as I want to be on those days. 

But I really do make up for it! I hustle on the lower levels, and I'm always willing to do the heavy lifting once I get my wind back. I just want to be a team player, you know? I hope my boss sees that I'm trying my best, even with this little quirk. That shows I'm still dedicated, right?

And, um, if an elevator or a construction lift is absolutely the only way up... I might ask the foreman if I can do different work on the ground instead. Um, I hope I'm not wasting your time, because it's probably not a huge deal, but... well, I'm here because I have this thing with elevators. \n\nI know they aren't really dangerous, right? I mean, I work in construction, so I know how things are built and I shouldn't be bothered by something like that. But when I have to get in one, I just... I get so scared, like I'm going to be trapped in there. I try to just push through it and tell myself it's fine, but I can't seem to make myself feel normal about it. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope that's the kind of thing you help with. I just feel a little silly even bringing it up, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar feelings of fear or anxiety in situations other than being in elevators?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, it's really just the elevators. Um... I don't have problems with anything else. Like, on the job site, I'm totally fine working in tight spaces or up high or whatever. I always do my job well, I promise. I don't let things get to me usually. \n\nIt's literally just this one weird thing. I hope that's an okay answer? Like, is it normal to just have one specific thing that bothers you like that, and everything else is totally fine?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself going out of your way to avoid elevators when possible?\n\nPatient: Well, um... maybe a little? But I don't make a big deal out of it, I promise. Most days I just... I take the stairs at my apartment, and on the job sites too. I usually just tell people I'm trying to get some extra steps in, you know? It's good exercise, so it's not like it's a bad thing. \n\nBut, um... if an elevator or a hoist is literally the only way up to a floor... I usually try to just ask the foreman if there's different work I can do on a lower level. I always make sure I'm still working hard when I do that, though! I never just sit around. Or, if I absolutely have to ride it, I'll... well, I'll only go if somebody I really trust is standing right there in the car with me. \n\nThat's not too crazy, right? I just feel better if someone is there. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee or anything... I really do try my best to just work around it without bothering anyone.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you are unable to avoid riding an elevator?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it gets a little intense, but it's really not the worst thing in the world, you know? I try to keep it together. But, um... as soon as those doors start sliding shut, it's like... my chest just gets really tight. And my hands start sweating, which is embarrassing, especially if I'm around the crew. \n\nI just... all I can think is, 'I need out,' even if we're only going up, like, a few floors. I know it's silly because it's just a short ride, and I always tell myself I'm fine. But in that moment, my body just kind of panics a little bit. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound weak or anything. I really do try to hide it so I don't bother anyone else. Is that a normal way to react when you're scared of something?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a job or an opportunity because it involved working in a building with elevators as the primary means of access?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I have, maybe once or twice? But I promise I'm still working really hard! I still go to all my shifts, and I'm still making it to my GED classes at night. I never just skip out on my responsibilities, you know? \n\nIt's just... taking the stairs everywhere can make me a little bit late sometimes, or I get pretty worn out before I even start my shift. And, um, I guess if I have to turn down a job because it's only accessible by an elevator or a hoist, it... it probably does make me a little less useful to the foreman on certain sites. \n\nI feel really bad about it, I do. I try to make up for it by working extra hard on the ground level stuff! I hope that's okay? Like, I'm still doing my best, I'm just... working around it a little bit. That doesn't make me a terrible worker, does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided social or professional events because they were being held in a location that you knew would require you to use an elevator?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really? I mean, I don't think I've ever skipped out on something important just because of that. I really try to be there for things, especially if it's for work or if friends invite me out. \n\nI might, um... I might just check ahead of time to see if there are stairs I can use instead? And if it's way up high, I'll usually just say I have a conflict or I'm too tired from work, which is kind of true sometimes! But I don't just sit at home avoiding people, I promise. I always try to make it work if I can. That's a pretty normal way to handle it, right? I just don't want to be a bother to anyone else if I get nervous.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid going to certain places, like shopping malls or hospitals, because they have elevators?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I do sometimes? But I try not to let it ruin my whole day or anything! Like, most days, I just take the stairs at my apartment building, and I do the same thing on job sites. It's actually really good exercise, you know? \n\nIf I have to go somewhere like a hospital or a mall, I'll usually just look for the stairs right away. And, um, if an elevator or a construction lift is absolutely the only way up... I might ask the foreman if I can do different work on the ground instead. Or... well, if I really, really have to ride it, I'll only do it if somebody I trust is standing right there in the car with me. \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too needy or annoying for the other guys on the crew. I really try to just handle it quietly so I'm not making a big fuss. Taking the stairs is a pretty healthy choice anyway, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of elevators is interfering with your daily life or overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess maybe a little bit? But I don't want it to sound like I'm falling apart or anything! Like I said, I'm still getting to all my construction shifts, and I haven't missed a single GED class. I'm really staying on top of my responsibilities! \n\nIt's just... I guess taking the stairs all the time does make me a little late sometimes, or I show up to my shift already pretty worn out from climbing. And, um, turning down those jobs where you have to use a hoist or an elevator... I suppose it does make me a little less useful to the foreman on certain assignments. \n\nBut I'm really trying my best to make up for it in other ways! I don't want anyone to think I'm slacking off or that I can't handle my life. It's just a small bump in the road, right? Lots of people have little things they have to work around, don't they?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're missing out on important experiences because of your fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: Well, um... no, I don't think so. I really don't feel like I'm missing out on anything big. I mean, my life is pretty full with work and my classes, and I'm really happy with how things are going! \n\nIf my friends want to do something, we usually just go to places on the ground floor anyway, like a diner or a park. And if there's ever a time we need to go up, I just find the stairs. It's really not a big deal to them, and it doesn't bother me either. \n\nI guess maybe I missed a couple of rooftop parties over the years, but I'm not really a big party person anyway, you know? I'd much rather just have a quiet night in or hang out somewhere easy. So I don't think it's holding me back from living a good life. That makes sense, right? I think I'm doing okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of elevators has caused you to significantly change your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, definitely not significantly. I mean, my routine is pretty much just like anyone else's, I think! I get up, go to the job site, head over to my classes... it's all very normal. \n\nI guess I just factor in a few extra minutes in the morning if I know I have to take the stairs somewhere, but that's just a habit now. It really doesn't feel like a big change or anything disruptive. I just wake up a tiny bit earlier, and then everything goes totally smooth. \n\nI really try to just blend in and do what I need to do without letting it mess up my schedule. I'm still super reliable! Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your questions right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of elevators has led you to avoid applying for jobs that you would otherwise be interested in, due to the building having elevators?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I wouldn't say that. I really like the jobs I get! I mean, construction is mostly outside or on lower levels anyway, especially the kind of labor I do. So it's not like I'm turning down all these amazing opportunities or anything.\n\nI guess, maybe, if a job was in a really tall high-rise and I knew I'd have to use the construction elevator every single day... I might look for a different site? But there's always plenty of work around, so it's not like I'm limiting myself. I just stick to what I know I can handle well, so I can be the best worker possible for the crew. \n\nI really wouldn't want a foreman to think I'm picky or ungrateful for the work. I'm definitely not! I just try to be practical. That's a good way to look at it, isn't it? I hope that doesn't make me sound difficult to work with.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of elevators is controlling a significant part of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... controlling? No, I wouldn't use that word. I really don't want it to sound like I'm just letting it ruin everything, because I'm not! \n\nI mean, I'm still getting to work every day, and I make it to my GED classes just fine. I'm keeping up with all my responsibilities. It’s just... well, I guess always having to take the stairs, or having to turn down those assignments where the only way up is a lift... it does take a little bit of a toll. \n\nClimbing all those flights can sometimes make me run a few minutes late, and I get pretty worn out before the heavy lifting even starts for the day. So, maybe I end up being a little less useful on some of those assignments than I want to be. \n\nBut I always try to work extra hard to make up for it once I catch my breath! I really don't want to be a burden on the rest of the crew. I just want to do a good job and be helpful. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad worker... I'm handling it okay, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of elevators has caused you to avoid more situations over time?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think it's gotten worse or spread to other things. I mean, it's really just the elevators, you know? \n\nI haven't started avoiding other stuff. I'm still doing everything I normally do. I take the subway just fine, I don't mind small rooms or anything like that. It's just that one specific thing with the lifts. \n\nI really try hard not to let things pile up or get out of hand. I just deal with the stairs and keep moving forward. I think I've managed to keep it pretty contained, honestly. That's a good sign, isn't it? That it hasn't spread to other parts of my life? I really hope that means I'm doing okay with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like avoiding elevators has significantly limited your career advancement in construction?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, no, I wouldn't say it's limited my advancement, exactly. I mean, I'm still working hard, getting to my job sites, going to my GED classes at night... I'm really trying to do everything right!\n\nI guess... like I was saying before, having to take the stairs all the time, or passing on those jobs where you have to take the lift up... it does mean I get there a little late sometimes, or I'm already out of breath before the real work starts. So maybe I can't take on certain roles that need someone up top right away, and I might not be quite as useful as I want to be on those days. \n\nBut I really do make up for it! I hustle on the lower levels, and I'm always willing to do the heavy lifting once I get my wind back. I just want to be a team player, you know? I hope my boss sees that I'm trying my best, even with this little quirk. That shows I'm still dedicated, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of elevators is affecting your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not! I really try my best to not let it bother anyone else. With my family and friends, it just... it never really comes up, honestly. We usually just hang out at our houses or places on the ground floor anyway, so it's not an issue. \n\nAnd with the crew at work... well, like I said, I make sure to hustle so they don't have to wait on me or pick up my slack. I'd just hate to be an inconvenience to them, you know? I always try to be friendly and pull my weight so nobody gets annoyed with me. They don't seem bothered by it... at least, I really hope they aren't. \n\nI think I do a pretty good job of keeping it to myself so it doesn't mess up anyone else's day. That's the right way to handle it, isn't it? I just want everyone to get along and to be a good friend and coworker.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of elevators has prevented you from pursuing a promotion or a new job opportunity?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think it's held me back from any new jobs or promotions. I mean, I'm just a laborer right now anyway, and I'm really focused on finishing my GED classes first before I try to move up too much. I want to make sure I'm really ready, you know?\n\nIf a job came up that needed me to ride the lift all the time... well, I guess I just wouldn't apply for that specific one. But there's plenty of work on the lower levels or outside! I always find a way to stay busy and be useful where I am. \n\nI really don't think it's stopping me from moving forward in life. I'm doing all the right things to better myself, like the night classes. I'm trying really hard to be responsible about my future. That's the most important thing, right? As long as I keep working hard where I can, it shouldn't matter too much, should it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, definitely not! I really wouldn't let something like this get in the way of spending time with the people I care about. Family and friends are so important, you know? 

Like I was saying earlier, we mostly just hang out at each other's houses or go to places on the ground floor anyway, like parks or restaurants. It just naturally works out that way, so it's never really been an issue. 

If there ever was a time someone wanted to go somewhere higher up, I'm sure I'd just take the stairs to meet them! I wouldn't want to miss out or make them change their plans just for me. I always try to be easygoing and go with the flow so everyone has a good time. That's the best way to handle things with friends, right? Just being flexible so nobody has to worry about you.

I just wake up a tiny bit earlier, and then everything goes totally smooth. \n\nI really try to just blend in and do what I need to do without letting it mess up my schedule.
